"5e6fca3dc76110b0c2828a969d3f760c1bca172497898590457292b23941e513", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:72000]\n=== PDF page 1 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence\nIntervals\nTOMAS KALIBERA, RICHARD JONES, University of Kent\nMeasuring performance and quantifying a performance change are core evaluation techniques in pro-\ngramming language and systems research. Out of 122 recent scientiﬁc papers published at PLDI, ASPLOS,\nISMM, TOPLAS, and TACO, as many as 65 included experimental evaluation that quantiﬁed a performance\nchange using a ratio of execution times. Unfortunately, few of these papers evaluated their results with the\nlevel of rigour that has come to be expected in other experimental sciences. The uncertainty of measured\nresults was largely ignored. Scarcely any of the papers mentioned uncertainty in the ratio of the mean exe-\ncution times, and most did not even mention uncertainty in the two means themselves. Furthermore, most\nof the papers failed to address the non-deterministic execution of computer programs (caused by factors such\nas memory placement, for example), and none addressed non-deterministic compilation (when a compiler\ncreates different binaries from the same sources, which differ in performance, for example again because\nof impact on memory placement). It turns out that the statistical methods presented in the computer sys-\ntems performance evaluation literature for the design and summary of experiments do not readily allow this\neither. This poses a hazard to the repeatability, reproducibility and even validity of quantitative results.\nInspired by statistical methods used in other ﬁelds of science, and building on results in statistics that\ndid not make it to introductory textbooks, we present a statistical model that allows us both to quantify\nuncertainty in the ratio of (execution time) means and to design experiments with a rigorous treatment of\nthose multiple sources of non-determinism that might impact measured performance. Better still, under\nour framework summaries can be as simple as “system A is faster than system B by 5.5% ± 2.5%, with\n95% conﬁdence”, a more natural statement than those derived from typical current practice, which are often\nmisinterpreted.\nCategories and Subject Descriptors: D.2.8 [Software Engineering]: Metrics—Performance measures; D.3.4\n[Programming Languages]: Processors—Run-time environments\nGeneral Terms: Experimentation, Measurement, Performance\nAdditional Key Words and Phrases: statistical methods, random effects, effect size\n1. INTRODUCTION\nQuantiﬁcation of performance change is a common task in experimental computer sci-\nence. Out of all 122 papers published in ASPLOS, ISMM, PLDI, TACO and TOPLAS in\n2011 up to 2nd August, 65 included empirical evaluation that quantiﬁed performance\nchange by giving the ratio of execution times (for example, speed-ups of optimisations,\nor overheads of new techniques). Quantiﬁcation of performance change is also part of\nthe software development process (for example, detecting performance regressions),\nboth open-source and commercial.\nExecution times for quantiﬁcation are obtained by running benchmark applications\nusing the two systems to compare. Computer systems are becoming more complex and\nincreasingly resemble the direct products of nature observed by physicists and natu-\nral scientists — an overwhelming number of factors inﬂuence performance. Some are\nunknown, some are out of the experimenter’s control, and some are non-deterministic.\nRepeated executions of the same benchmark on the same system, even when the exper-\nimenter does everything possible to enforce the same conditions, always report slightly\ndifferent execution times. This is even more pronounced if the same experiment is re-\npeated by an independent experimenter using different equipment. Thus, for credible\nAuthor’s addresses: T. Kalibera (R.E. Jones), School of Computing, University of Kent, Canterbury, CT2 7NF,\nUK.\nA preliminary version of a portion of this work was presented at the Third European Performance Engineering\nWorkshop.\narXiv:2007.10899v1  [stat.ME]  21 Jul 2020\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\n2\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\nquantiﬁcation of performance change, one needs to design experiments and summarize\ntheir results in a repeatable and reproducible manner. Experiment design and statis-\ntical inference are the ﬁelds of statistics that address these issues, and are widely\napplied in physics, natural sciences and social sciences.\nUnfortunately, the practice in computer science lags behind. Of the 122 papers at\nASPLOS, ISMM, PLDI, TACO, and TOPLAS we looked at, 90 evaluated execution\ntime based on experiments. This includes ratio of execution times for two systems and\nalso the execution times for a single system. 71 of these 90 papers completely ignored\nthe question of uncertainty in the measured times. This lack of rigour makes repeata-\nbility difﬁcult and undermines the validity of the results. We hardly dare mention that\nsimple rules for summarizing and reporting uncertainties are taught to every student\nof physics and natural sciences [Kirkup 1994], are part of engineering practice [Taylor\nand Kuyatt 1994], and are based on statistics taught in introductory courses. More-\nover, advice on elementary experiment design and statistical inference has been read-\nily available to computer scientists in the form of textbooks aimed and used for teach-\ning [Jain 1991; Lilja 2000]. A subset of the statistics of the latter textbook has been\nadvocated in the context of Java runtime performance evaluation [Georges et al. 2007]\nand compared with the practice in scientiﬁc papers, with results not particularly ﬂat-\ntering to our ﬁeld.\nNevertheless, even though the best quantiﬁcation method recommended so far in our\nﬁeld ([Georges et al. 2007] based largely on [Lilja 2000]) is ahead of current practice,\nit has a number of ﬂaws. It exacts a high price for statistical rigour in summarizing\nresults: we do not get a reliable estimate of the metric that we are ultimately interested\nin, which is the ratio of execution times. Maybe this ﬂaw on its own is a signiﬁcant\ndetractor from wider adoption of the method in practice. Even worse, the rigour that\nthe method does provide is highly questionable, as the method is based on statistical\nsigniﬁcance, a concept with a number of shortcomings, some of which have been known\nfor seven decades [Cohen 1994]. While statistical signiﬁcance still dominates elemen-\ntary statistical textbooks and is still used by some researchers even in other ﬁelds,\nits limits have been well described and arguments for its deprecation have been made\nin statistics [Royall 1986], psychology [Cohen 1994], education [Coe 2002], ecology,\nmedicine, bio-medicine, and biology [Nakagawa and Cuthill 2007]. Critical views of\nstatistical signiﬁcance have also been published in the context of research in sociology,\ncriminology, economics, marketing, chemistry, and nursing (see Fidler and Cumming\n[2007] for references and details). Some journals explicitly require alternative meth-\nods [Hill and Thompson 2005]. And such methods are available. As we demonstrate\nin this paper, the specialized statistical literature [Fieller 1954; Davison and Hinkley\n1997] provides methods to estimate the ratio of execution times we are interested in —\ninference of the ratio of means is possible, and a so-called effect size conﬁdence interval\ncan be constructed for it.\nApart from the problems with the summary of results, Georges et al.’s quantiﬁcation\nmethod, while ahead of the prevailing practice in the ﬁeld, lacks rigour in experimen-\ntal design. It is based on a two-level 1 hierarchical experiment that repeats executions\nof a benchmark, each consisting of repeated measurements. For example, to evaluate\nan optimisation in a Java virtual machine, the method requires us to run many invoca-\ntions of the JVM and have each invocation run and measure many iterations of a test\napplication. Two levels are not enough. It is well known that a large number of factors\nthat inﬂuence performance are inherently non-deterministic or need to be randomized\nby experimenter to avoid measurement bias [Mytkowicz et al. 2009]. The ﬁrst group\nincludes for example context switches, hardware interrupts, memory placement due\n1Please note that we use the term ‘level’ informally. In our text, it is not what‘factor level’ means in statistics.\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n3\nto virtual-to-physical memory mapping [Kalibera et al. 2005], randomized algorithms\nin compilation [Kalibera and Tuma 2006], or decisions of a just-in-time compiler on\nwhich methods to compile. Non-deterministic compilation by itself means that compi-\nlation needs to be repeated. The second group includes symbol names of methods and\nvariables in source code which impacts binary code layout [Gu et al. 2004], the size of\nthe UNIX process environment or the linking order [Mytkowicz et al. 2009].\nTo efﬁciently run experiments with such as number of sources of non-determinism,\nwe need more than two levels. Georges et al.’s quantiﬁcation method, however, does\nnot allow this. It is, in fact, based on one-level only summarization, which looks at\nthe means from the lowest level (repeated measurements) instead of the measure-\nment themselves. There is a better way. While not included in elementary statistical\ntextbooks, there are statistical methods which model so-called random effects [Charles\nE. McCulloch 2008], which can represent non-deterministic factors in our experiments.\nTo our knowledge, however, none of the existing models can be used directly. Thus, we\nextend one such model to be more robust and to support an arbitrary number of exper-\niment levels. We also derive the optimum number of repetitions at each level (with the\nbest recommended method so far, there is no systematic guidance to select the number\nof repetitions).\nOur contributions in this work are:\n(1) Statistical Inference for Quantiﬁcation. A statistical model of a hierarchy of\nrandom effects that contribute to ﬂuctuations in performance. This allows us to\nbuild a conﬁdence interval for the mean performance metric (such as execution\ntime) within such a hierarchy. This is our ﬁrst original contribution to statistics.\n(2) Experiment Planning for Quantiﬁcation. After some initial measurements,\nthe model can also be used to guide the experiment. Repeating all experiments\nblindly is often very expensive. However, the model will give the optimum number\nof repetitions of experiments at different levels of the hierarchy that are necessary\nto provide the narrowest conﬁdence interval in available experimentation time.\nThis is our second original contribution to statistics.\n(3) Asymptotic Parametric Quantiﬁcation Method. A parametric approach to\nconstructing a conﬁdence interval for the ratio of mean metric values, based on\nFieller’s theorem [Fieller 1954], asymptotic normality of the mean, and our statis-\ntical model. Hence, for example, the result of a study might be that the change is\n5.5% ± 2.5%, with 95% conﬁdence. Such a statement provides a clear but rigorous\naccount of the magnitude of the performance change and the uncertainty involved.\n(4) Bootstrap Quantiﬁcation Method. An alternative non-parametric approach to\nconstructing the same conﬁdence interval using hierarchical random re-sampling\n(a bootstrap method). This is an application of an existing statistical method, used\nin our ﬁeld much less than it deserves.\n(5) Evaluation. Thorough evaluation of the two approaches on a set of benchmarks.\nWe estimate the true coverage of the intervals that can be obtained. We also es-\ntimate a false alarm rate (the case where a change is detected although there is\nnone), given a threshold of how large changes need to be for us to care. A particu-\nlarly important beneﬁt of our method is that it supports a rigorous way to use such\na threshold. This is our third original contribution.\nContributions 1, 2, 4, and partially 3 and 5 also apply to evaluations of a single sys-\ntem. For contributions 1 and 2, we extend and correct a preliminary version published\nin [Kalibera and Tuma 2006], which in turn extends [Kalibera et al. 2005]. For item\n5, we use our benchmark data from [Kalibera and Tuma 2006] as inputs for (new)\nstatistical simulations.\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\n4\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\nTable I. Current Practice of Performance Quantiﬁcation\nPLDI\nASPLOS\nISMM\nTOPLAS\nTACO\nTotal\nNumber of Papers\n55\n32\n13\n13\n9\n122\nEvaluated Execution Time\n42 (28)\n25 (20)\n12 (11)\n5 (2)\n6 (6)\n90 (67)\nIgnored Uncertainty In Measurement\n39 (24)\n18 (12)\n5 (5)\n4 (1)\n5 (5)\n71 (47)\nEvaluated Ratio of Execution Times\n27 (25)\n22 (19)\n9 (8)\n1 (1)\n6 (6)\n65 (59)\nSource: Scientiﬁc papers at selected venues published in 2011, up to 2nd August. Counts are given in form\n‘A (M)’, where A is the number of all papers that reported the metric, and M the number of papers where\nthe metric was important or the main result in the evaluation.\n2. BACKGROUND: CURRENT QUANTIFICATION METHODS\nIn this section, we provide an overview of quantiﬁcation methods currently used, we\ndescribe their limitations, and we summarize the best-so-far quantiﬁcation methods\nrecommended for overcoming these limitations.\n2.1. Currently Used Methods\nTo map the current quantiﬁcation methods used in computer science, we analysed pa-\npers published at selected conferences (PLDI, ASPLOS, ISMM) and journals (TOPLAS,\nTACO). We restricted our survey to papers published between 1st January and 2nd Au-\ngust, 2011. We thus cover all papers from the conferences, issues 1–4 of TOPLAS, and\nissues 1–2 of TACO — 122 papers in total.\nAs summarized in Table I, nearly three quarters of the papers studied included em-\npirical performance evaluation that measured and reported execution time. The major-\nity of the execution time evaluations (over 70%) reported the ratio of execution times\nas a measure of performance change (the remaining 30% reported execution time for\na single system). In total, over half of all papers reported the ratio of execution times.\nWe also noted papers where execution time was important for the evaluation (counts\nin parentheses in the table). More than half of the papers had empirical evaluations\nwith execution time as an important metric. Out of these papers where execution time\nwas an important metric, in nearly 90% it was the ratio of execution times that was\nreported (only in 10% of cases it was the absolute execution time for a single system).\nNearly half of all papers had empirical evaluation with ratio of execution times as an\nimportant metric. The ratio of execution times thus seems to be a metric that people\ncare about and report. Anecdotal evidence conﬁrms that this metric is also important\nto industrial developers.\nIn practice, where decisions have to be made based on the results of performance\nquantiﬁcation, there are certain minimum thresholds for relative performance change\nabove/below which it is considered of no practical interest. These thresholds may de-\npend on the evaluation context (a particular system, regression test, optimisation, new\nfeature). These thresholds are also assumed and used in Georges et al. [2007]. Of the\npapers in our survey, thresholds on ratio of execution times are explicitly part of an\nauto-tuning algorithm by Singer et al. [2011], where they have to be set (manually,\nbased on experience) large enough to cater for variabilities in the data. However, most\npapers that quantify performance change report only the ratio of (mean) execution\ntimes, and thus imply that this ratio alone, maybe along with an understanding of the\ncontext of the study, is sufﬁcient to assess importance of a performance change. The use\nof ‘thresholds’ to assess importance is implied. We also have conﬁrmation from a senior\nmember of a well-known industrial research laboratory of the use of such thresholds\nin industry.\nNearly 80% of the papers studied that evaluated execution time (both absolute and\nrelative) failed to mention anything about uncertainty in the ﬁgures they reported.\nThe numbers are not that much better if we only focus on papers where execution\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n5\ntime was an important metric: 70% of these papers failed to mention uncertainty. Note\nthat we were very generous in the classiﬁcation — if a paper reported that uncertainty\nwas low, without any further details, we gave it the beneﬁt of the doubt and counted\nit as having taken uncertainty into account. Ignoring uncertainty represents a severe\nlack of rigour in face of the fact that execution time on today’s computer systems is\nalways subject to variation in performance. While majority of the papers studied ran\nexperiments on real hardware, it should be mentioned that some (particularly from\nASPLOS) were based on execution time measurements in a simulator. It is certainly\npossible to have a simulator in which execution time is deterministic, and we did not\nattempt to check the particular simulators used in individual papers we analysed.\nHowever, if a simulator is deterministic, it is not a realistic one [Alameldeen and Wood\n2003]. Thus, we have not attempted to extract simulations from the summary — using\na deterministic simulator in our opinion falls into the category of ignoring uncertainty.\nWe used to hear arguments that, for very large speed-ups (say 2×), summarizing\nuncertainty is a waste of effort. This implies that experimenter experience is that, in\nthe given system, uncertainty would be much smaller. However, in systems research\nmuch smaller performance changes are usually reported. Mytkowicz et al. [2009] found\nthat out of 88 papers from ASPLOS 2008, PACT 2007, PLDI 2007, CGO 2007 with ex-\nperimental evaluation in a dedicated section, the median of reported speed-ups was\nonly 10%. In their work they show that the measurement bias with SPEC CPU 2006\nbenchmarks can easily obfuscate this speed-up when evaluating compiler optimisa-\ntions. This means proper randomization to avoid this bias would have lead to higher\nuncertainty than 10%, and hence proper handling of this uncertainty would be neces-\nsary (such a randomization tool was later provided by Curtsinger and Berger [2012]). A\nsenior member of a well-known industrial research lab told us that he would care only\nabout differences of 10% for new work, but look for differences of as little as 2-3% or\neven 1% when looking for regressions (where he would look at uncertainty too). Since\nsuch small performance changes are often of interest, ignoring uncertainty in systems\nresearch represents a serious threat to validity. When speed-ups are large compared\nto expected uncertainty, validity would not be threatened, but ignoring it would still\nbe a serious lack of rigour and in our view not a property of good research.\nOut of the 19 papers that touched on uncertainty in any way, most reported un-\ncertainty of single systems in the form of a standard deviation or of 95% conﬁdence\nintervals. It was exceptional for a paper to specify how the conﬁdence intervals were\nconstructed; most were presumably based on the normal distribution. While most pa-\npers reported the ratio of execution times, they mostly showed uncertainty only for\nthe means in isolation of each of the two systems compared. Only three of the papers\nreported the uncertainty of the ratios. However, it seems from the text of two of these\nthat they did not take uncertainty of both systems into account, but only normalized\nthe uncertainty of the new system against the mean of the old system. The third pa-\nper was ours, and it took the uncertainty of both systems into account, but we have to\nadmit that our text did not say that. The remaining papers which address uncertainty\ndo so by saying that it was low (without giving numbers), and one says that a proposed\noptimisation reduced it (without giving numbers).\nThe papers we studied gave extremely little information on the experimental design\nused. From some papers, it is not even obvious if benchmarks were executed more than\nonce, and hence if the reported execution time is not just a single measurement. Only\nvery few papers mention that their experiments repeat at two levels (i.e. execution of\na benchmark and measurement within that benchmark). However, all of those that do\nalso seem to treat the data as if it came from a one-level experiment. If executions were\nrepeated, the numbers of repetitions seemed to be arbitrary. Some experiments used\nan adaptive number of iterations based on a heuristic — the benchmark code itself\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\n6\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\ncalculates the standard deviation of the last few measurements taken and terminates\nthe experiment once the standard deviation is sufﬁciently small.\nSome papers claim to use certain evaluation methodologies, such as those of\nMytkowicz et al. [2009], Georges et al. [2007] or Georges et al. [2008]. However, these\npapers rarely made it clear how the concrete experiments were performed. One paper\nclaims to use the method proposed by Mytkowicz et al. to reduce variance, without any\nfurther details. However, the cited paper argues for dealing with measurement bias\nthrough randomization, which in fact increases the variance, but for very good rea-\nsons. Some papers mention uncertainty due to the decisions of a just-in-time compiler\nof which methods to compile. Conﬁdence intervals or standard deviation error bars\nare usually shown for a single system with one particular conﬁguration, even when\nmultiple conﬁgurations were used. Uncertainty quantiﬁed in this way is prone to bias.\nSummary. The ratio of (mean) execution times is the major metric for quantiﬁcation of\nperformance change. A threshold for the minimum/maximum ratio is used as a mea-\nsure of the practical importance of a difference. Uncertainty in the ratio of execution\ntimes is almost never quantiﬁed, and when it is, only in an unclear way. Sometimes\nseparate uncertainties for the two systems (means) are shown, but more commonly\neven these are ignored. Benchmarks are mostly repeated only at one level, or excep-\ntionally at two levels (executions and measurements). Two-level experiments, if done\nat all, are evaluated as if they were one-level only (we believe by merging all the mea-\nsurements from the different executions, but the texts are not explicit here).\n2.2. What Is Wrong With Currently Used Methods\nAny paper that reports execution time, yet ignores the uncertainty of the ﬁnal met-\nric, be it absolute execution time of one system or the ratio of execution times for two\nsystems, is methodologically ﬂawed. Moreover, failing to address uncertainty makes\nthe results unrepeatable. Repeating any experiment will lead to slightly different re-\nsults due to variability in measurement. Without quantiﬁed uncertainty, we cannot\nknow if we got a ‘slightly’ different result due to variability in measurement, or a very\ndifferent result due to an error. Note that a failure to address uncertainty is not the\nonly way to get unrepeatable results. A common problem is measurement bias, when\nthe experimenter fails to identify and then describe or vary a factor that largely inﬂu-\nences the results [Mytkowicz et al. 2009]. In such a case, statistics even properly used\nfor factors that were varied cannot help and the summarized uncertainty would be\nmisleadingnly small. The responsibility for identifying these factors lies fully on the\nexperimenter and no statistics can help. But even when important factors are ﬁxed\nand described or varied properly, failing to address uncertainty is a potential threat\nto validity when quantifying performance change, and this threat is particularly high\nwhen the observed change is relatively small (e.g. 10%), which it often is. When such\nquantiﬁcation is used to show that an optimisation provides a good speed-up, there is\na risk that the ‘improvement’ lies within the range of uncertainty. A similar risk ap-\nplies to some extent when quantiﬁcation is used to show that the overhead of a certain\nfeature is small — if the uncertainty of the new system is much larger than the old\none, the price may in practice be high even if the mean overhead is small.\nFailure to report how many repetitions of a benchmark were executed makes re-\npeatability harder. Often, the reader is only left with suspicion that insufﬁcient repeti-\ntions were made and in particular that the researchers did not repeat whole executions\nof benchmarks (that in turn repeat measurements within a single execution). Some-\ntimes, the reported number of measurements used for averaging is suspiciously small\n(e.g. three or ﬁve). Failure to repeat enough times, or indeed to repeat at all, increases\nthe risk of obtaining unrepeatable results and reaching incorrect conclusions.\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n7\nIn summary, our survey of published papers suggests that the current practice of\nquantiﬁcation of performance change in scientiﬁc papers is weak. We are not the\nﬁrst to ﬁnd this, but we were surprised at how prevalent this practice is even in the\nmost recent papers. The lack of reporting of uncertainties while quantifying a perfor-\nmance change in Java runtimes research was documented by Georges et al. [2007].\nMytkowicz et al. [2009] discovered that measurement bias was not addressed in ex-\nperimental computer systems performance evaluation; this is a more general problem,\nbut it also applies to the quantiﬁcation of performance change. These shortcomings\nhave arisen despite well-known textbooks on computer systems performance evalua-\ntion [Jain 1991; Lilja 2000] having described a number of errors common in perfor-\nmance evaluation.\n2.3. Currently Proposed Methods\nExperimental design and statistical inference are mature ﬁelds of mathematical statis-\ntics with many results and described in a number of texts. Basic recommendations on\nconducting experiments in physics and natural sciences, measuring uncertainties, cal-\nculating propagation of uncertainties, and reporting the results are available in text-\nbooks such as [Kirkup 1994]. More advanced rules of propagation of uncertainties can\nbe found in a recommendation by NIST [Taylor and Kuyatt 1994]. High-level overview\nand pointers to works on experiment design and the scientiﬁc method in general can\nbe found in [Wilson 1952]. While physics does not deal with exactly the same problems\nas experimental computer science, many ideas are general. Moreover, basic methods of\nstatistical inference and experimental design, in the context of computer systems per-\nformance evaluation, have then been presented in books [Jain 1991; Lilja 2000]. These\nbooks have been and are being used as textbooks for teaching performance evalua-\ntion courses. Georges et al. [2007] argued for wider adoption of a performance change\nquantiﬁcation method based on Lilja’s book, in the context of performance evaluation\nof Java runtimes. They also added some practical recommendations for the number of\nrepetitions of benchmark measurements.\nIn this section, we summarize the current recommended best practice for quantiﬁ-\ncation of performance change, based on Jain [1991], Lilja [2000], and Georges et al.\n[2007].\n2.3.1. Experiment Design. Georges et al. [2007] recommends repeating measurements\nwithin a single benchmark, as well as repeating executions of the whole benchmark.\nThe number of measurements within a benchmark is controlled by a heuristic in the\nbenchmark — as soon as the standard deviation of the last few measurements is\nsmall enough, the benchmark’s execution is ﬁnished. The number of benchmark execu-\ntions is an arbitrary choice of the experimenter, or, alternatively, additional executions\nare adaptively added until conﬁdence interval for the mean is sufﬁciently narrow (its\nwidth is within 1% or 2% of the sample mean).\nLilja [2000] and particularly Jain [1991] also recommend experimental designs that\ncope with various combinations of factors that can be ﬁxed by the experimenter. These\ndesigns apply to factors that can take only a ﬁnite, usually small, number of values\n(i.e. a particular processor or benchmark). The effect of choosing a particular value\n(say the processor) on performance is of interest to the experimenter. Such effects are\ncalled ﬁxed effects in statistics (see [Charles E. McCulloch 2008] and [Searle et al.\n1992] for more detailed discussion of ﬁxed and random effects). The experimental de-\nsigns then help in planning which combinations of values of which factors to run to\nget reasonable information given reasonable experimentation time. The methods rec-\nommended by Jain [1991] and Lilja [2000], however, do not address effects of factors\nthat can take a large number of values, usually at random and often not under our\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\n8\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\ncontrol, for which the performance effect of a particular choice is not interesting. Such\na factor can be, say, a particular mapping of virtual to physical pages chosen by the\noperating system. In statistics, these are called random effects. In this work, we only\naddress random effects. Our work could be extended to include ﬁxed effects, as there\nindeed are statistical models that include both types of effects [Charles E. McCulloch\n2008], but we do not attempt it here.\nGeorges et al. [2008] proposes a particular experiment design for performance eval-\nuations within managed environments with just-in-time compilation. The design as-\nsumes generation of several compilation plans and then running both systems using\nthese plans. The motivation for this design, in contrast to just keeping the compila-\ntion plans random (unpaired) for the two systems, is smaller uncertainty within given\nexperimentation time.\n2.3.2. Summary of Experiment Results. A performance change is quantiﬁed using statis-\ntical signiﬁcance [Georges et al. 2007; Lilja 2000; Jain 1991]. The output is a binary\ndecision: “the systems (may) have the same performance” or “(it is likely that) the sys-\ntems do not have the same performance”. This decision is based on the probability that\nthe actually observed difference or larger in (sample) means of the two systems would\noccur if the (true) means were the same. This probability is called the p-value in statis-\ntical tests. The p-value is compared against a pre-deﬁned threshold called signiﬁcance\nlevel (i.e. 5%). If the p-value is smaller, then the second decision, that the systems do\nnot perform the same, is chosen. Otherwise, the ﬁrst decision, the default that they\nmay have the same performance, is chosen.\nThere are two recommended alternative methods that provide this kind of quantiﬁ-\ncation. The preferred one is a visual test using conﬁdence intervals. The other one is\na statistical test. The visual test is as follows. We construct two conﬁdence intervals\nfor the means of the two systems. We check if they overlap. If they do not, the decision\nis that “it is likely that the systems do not have the same performance”. If they do\noverlap, the method recommended by Lilja [2000] and Georges et al. [2007] ﬁnishes,\nconcluding that “the systems may have the same performance”. The method by Jain\n[1991] adds another step — it falls back to a statistical test if the intervals overlap only\nslightly, that is if centre of neither interval lies within the other. The visual test is pre-\nferred, because in addition to the binary decision, it is easy to give a visual measure of\nhow large the difference in the means actually is, compared to their uncertainty. While\nthis is a clear aid for an analyst, it lacks a rigorous semantics (and in his dissertation,\nGeorges [2008] recommends using the statistical test instead).\nThe conﬁdence interval used for the visual test has the same underlying statisti-\ncal backing as the statistical test. It is assumed that measurements are independent\nidentically distributed and follow the normal distribution (the normality assumption\nis incorrectly omitted in many texts as we discuss in greater detail in Section 3.2). Jain\n[1991] and Lilja [2000] suggest using Student’s t-test for unequal variances (or the cor-\nresponding interval). Georges et al. [2007] additionally recommend (an interval based\non) the z-test for sample sizes over 30. Georges et al. also suggest working with sample\nmeans of measurements from individual executions, instead of with the measurements\nthemselves. The corresponding test/interval is hence only for these sample means. An\nalternative solution that is sometimes used, but not part of their recommendation, is\nto join all the measurements from the different executions and treat them as coming\nfrom a one-level experiment.\nLilja [2000] and particularly Jain [1991] then describe analysis of variance (ANOVA)\nmethods to summarize experiments where multiple ﬁxed factors are varied (i.e. pro-\ncessor, memory size, etc.). Georges et al. [2007] summarizes also some of the ANOVA\nmethods from [Lilja 2000], but does not recommend it for use in (Java) performance\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n9\nevaluations as the outputs are too hard to understand and doing all the measurements\nto provide the inputs is too time consuming. We do not address these types of factors\n(‘ﬁxed effects’) in this work, but our method should be extensible to do so.\nThe summarisation method that Georges et al. [2008] recommends for the replay\ncompilation design leads to a test/interval based on paired t-test. The inputs are dif-\nferences of means obtained from the old and new systems, averaging over multiple ex-\necutions with a given compilation plan. We do not address paired comparisons in this\nwork, but most of our criticism of the recommended quantiﬁcation using the test/inter-\nval we mention later applies to it as well.\n3. WHAT IS WRONG WITH THE CURRENTLY PROPOSED METHODS\nEven though the current recommended best practice for quantiﬁcation of performance\nchange by [Jain 1991], [Lilja 2000], and [Georges et al. 2007] is ahead of the pitiful\npractice in the ﬁeld, it has a number of problems.\n3.1. Experiment Design\nRepetition of executions (and measurements) is not enough. It is necessary to include\nall inherently random factors in the experiment, and hence to repeat experiments at\nlevel(s) even higher than execution of the benchmark. For example, randomized com-\npilation necessitates both compilation and running the experiments for different bina-\nries. We have found this to be essential in our earlier work with the GNU C++ Com-\npiler when benchmarking CORBA middleware and when benchmarking applications\nwithin the Mono platform [Kalibera and Tuma 2006]. The empirical evaluation in this\npaper conﬁrms this need on the Mono platform. Furthermore, we need to randomize\nand include additional (non-random) factors that it does not make sense to ﬁx. These\nfactors can arise from the operating system or the language environment. Mytkowicz\net al. [2009] provide evidence that unexpected factors such as the size of UNIX process\nenvironment or the linking order can impact performance. Gu et al. [2004] observed\na performance impact of the names of identiﬁers in the source code. It is obvious that\nmany additional factors of this kind have not yet been discovered to have signiﬁcant\nperformance impact, so they cannot be really ﬁxed in experiments. Sufﬁcient random-\nization, called for by Mytkowicz et al. [2009], is hence necessary. Curtsinger and Berger\n[2012] provide a randomization tool applicable to applications studied in [Mytkowicz\net al. 2009].\nNote in particular that randomization of identiﬁer names or linking order leads in\nturn again to randomized compilation/building. Repeating compilation or repeating at\nan even higher level can be very expensive, because large systems take long to compile.\nRepeating too many times is a waste of resources, but repeating too few times under-\nmines repeatability and validity. Hence, a good experiment design method should also\nallow the researcher to derive an optimum number of repetitions at different levels, so\nas to make efﬁcient use of the time available for experimentation in order to reduce\nuncertainty in the ﬁnal result. The present recommended best practice does not offer\nthis.\n3.2. Summarization of Experiment Results\nThe single most signiﬁcant problem of the best recommended method is that it does\nnot tell us what we want to know. It does not give us the metric we are ultimately\ninterested in, a reliable estimate of the ratio of execution times. Of course, we can\nuse the recommended method and, in the case that the (binary) conclusion is that the\nsystems are likely to differ in performance, we can in addition report the ratio of the\nsample means. But the recommended method would not give us the uncertainty of this\nratio, so we do not know how much of the ratio is due to uncertainty. Since we do not\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\n10\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\nreport uncertainty of the ratio, the results we provide are unrepeatable and may not\nbe valid. This problem also means that the recommended method does not allow us to\ncompare the ratio against a threshold (the minimal/maximal change that is important\nto us).\nThe problem of not supporting comparison against a threshold is more signiﬁcant\nthan it may seem at ﬁrst — the recommended method looks for a performance differ-\nence however small as long as it is unlikely to be by pure chance. However, the larger\nthe sample size is (the more measurements we have), the more unlikely even a very\nsmall difference becomes. Hence, the decision of the method is inﬂuenced by the num-\nber of measurements. In practice this means that with a large sample size (and in our\nﬁeld it is easy to generate very large samples), the decision will nearly always be “it\nis likely that the systems do not have the same performance”, no matter how small\nor large the difference actually is. The method then becomes of very little use — it\njust adds an illusion of rigour to the results and, worse, only to the results we are not\ninterested in.\nThe fact that this does not tell us what we want, and that it often gives the same\nanswer only because the sample size is very large, is a fundamental drawback to the\nstatistical signiﬁcance. This drawback has been known for the last seven decades [Co-\nhen 1994]. It has been reiterated by researchers in psychology [Cohen 1994], educa-\ntion [Coe 2002], and more recently in bio-medicine and biology [Nakagawa and Cuthill\n2007]. This and other drawbacks of the method have been brought up in criticism of\nstatistical signiﬁcance also in sociology, criminology, economics, marketing, chemistry,\nand nursing (see Fidler and Cumming [2007] for references and details). The draw-\nbacks, however, seem to be quite unknown to experimental computer scientists. Even\nworse, methods based on statistical signiﬁcance are notoriously hard to interpret. This\nmay be partially because they do not give us the answer to what we want to know, but\nthey also offer temptations, such as the belief that the p-value is actually the proba-\nbility that the systems have the same performance. The interpretation of the results\nof statistical tests is so difﬁcult that even statistical textbooks sometimes get it wrong\n(examples are given by Cohen [1994]). In a study by Oakes [1986] cited by Cohen\n[1994], 68 of 70 psychologists made an error in the interpretation of a statistical test.\nInterpretation of the visual test using conﬁdence intervals seems simpler, but it is not\nmuch so. This is well reﬂected by the wording used and stressed by Lilja [2000], and\nrepeated by Georges et al. [2007] and Georges et al. [2008], to describe the positive\noutcome (the systems differ in performance) of the visual test — “there is no evidence\nto suggest that there is not a statistically signiﬁcant difference”.\nThe wording is rather cryptic as it tries to be precise, which we do not ﬁnd it to be.\nIn frequentist statistics which the texts rely on, the wording is incorrect. The goal of\nthe comparison is to learn something about the difference in the true but unknown\nmeans of two random variables. The true means are either equal or different, and\nthe statistical signiﬁcance does not speak about this difference (it speaks about the\ndifference of the sample means instead). Hence, the second part of the wording, “sta-\ntistically signiﬁcant difference” is incorrect. The ﬁrst part of the wording that speaks\nabout non-existence of evidence is incorrect as well. The test is about verifying if the\ndata we have measured are unlikely provided the true unknown means are the same.\nHence, we are checking if the data we have form an evidence against the assumed zero\nperformance change. We can make no claims about non-existence of evidence. And we\nare not looking for evidence for the true means being equal, but rather against it.\nLilja [2000] (also cited by Georges et al. [2007]) explain the wording by stating that\nthere is always a certain probability, ‘α’, that a large observed difference was due to\nrandom ﬂuctuations. This is correct, but does not justify the wording in our opinion.\nEven more, the context in the texts suggests that in the visual test of conﬁdence inter-\n\n\n=== PDF page 11 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n11\nval overlap, this probability (α) is the signiﬁcance of the conﬁdence intervals used (i.e.\nα = 0.05 for 95% conﬁdence interval), which it is not. In fact, with the visual test this\nprobability is not known. If we use 95% conﬁdence intervals, the probability of such an\nerror is not 5%, but under the normality assumption is below 1% [Payton et al. 2003].\nThis makes the visual test far more conservative than it may seem, and hence its re-\nsults are even harder to interpret, and more likely to mislead. Also, one would need far\nmore experimentation time to show a performance change. Further information on the\nerror of the visual test can be found in Payton et al. [2003; Schenker and Gentleman\n[2001].\nWith the (non-visual) statistical test, the theory tells us the probability of erro-\nneously concluding that compared systems do not have the same performance when\nactually they do. The probability of this error is the threshold we are comparing the\np-value against (often 5%). For this reason, Schenker and Gentleman [2001] (cited\nby Georges [2008]) prefer statistical test over the visual test. Our understanding is\nthat the visual test is generally preferred, though, in our ﬁeld, and that both method\nshare their key problems.\nYet another issue of visual and non-visual test is the use of parametric methods on\ndata that violate their assumptions. Computer performance measurements cannot be\nassumed to be normally distributed. Often they are multi-modal, with long-tails to\nthe right. Deviations from normality may not be fatal for the t-test/conﬁdence inter-\nval though, and some practitioners in other ﬁelds ignore them as well. The sample\nmean is asymptotically normal due to the Central Limit Theorem. Many texts in our\nﬁeld omit the normality assumption of the t-test or incorrectly state that the Central\nLimit Theorem is enough to overcome it ([Lilja 2000; Jain 1991; Georges et al. 2007]).\nThe Central Limit Theorem is not enough, but some more involved studies show that,\nunder certain deviations from normality, the parametric methods work well, even for\nreasonably small sample sizes (see for example Basu and DasGupta [1995] or Rasch\nand Guiard [2004]) given a reasonably high conﬁdence (95% conﬁdence intervals and\nwider). Still, there is no general agreement that ignoring the normality assumption\nis an acceptable practice, one should certainly make it clear if such assumptions are\nignored. Robust statistical methods do exist (see Erceg-Hurn and Mirosevich [2008]\nfor summary and references), and there has been no study of how the t-test/conﬁdence\ninterval is affected by violations from normality common in computer performance\ndata.\n4. HOW TO DO THINGS BETTER\nA good quantiﬁcation method needs to provide an estimate of the ratio of execution\ntime means and an estimate of its uncertainty. These estimates should be based on\nexperiments that include all random factors to which the measured system is subject,\nboth factors naturally random and factors that we randomize to avoid measurement\nbias. A statistical model of such an experiment can then offer some way of planning\nthe experiment: how many repetitions are needed at each level.\n4.1. Experiment Design with Random Effects\nThere are factors that inﬂuence performance, which are inherently random and we\ncannot control them. To get valid results (avoid bias), we need to repeat experiments\nat a high enough level to include all random factors. For example, if our system is prone\nto randomized compilation which has an effect on performance, we need to repeat com-\npilations and measure multiple binaries. With managed runtimes, the ‘binary’ may be\nthe binary of the virtual machine or the byte-code of the application. If we took an\nextreme position, it would be all compiled code that inﬂuences performance observed\nby the benchmark. In theory, we could start from the top level for each measurement,\n\n\n=== PDF page 12 ===\n12\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\nthus compiling the binary, executing it only once and running only one iteration (say\nafter dropping the initial measurements that were prone to warm-up noise). This ap-\nproach would allow use of a one-level model. However, it would be an extravagant\nwaste of time if the variation in binaries had a far smaller effect on performance than\nthe variation in, say, executions or measurements. In this case, intuitively it should be\npossible to do better by executing each binary multiple times and reporting more than\none measurement in an execution.\nThis situation can be modeled mathematically by a random effect models with n-way\nclassiﬁcation. Such random effects model is based on a hierarchy of n random ways,2\nwhich have effect on the random distribution of the actual measurements. Hence, a\nmodel with n-way classiﬁcation corresponds to an n+1 level experiment. In particular,\na three-level experiment with repeated compilations, executions, and measurements\ncorresponds to a model in 2-way classiﬁcation (measurements inﬂuenced by execution\nand compilation). Random effects models are sometimes also referred to as random\neffects (n-factor) ANOVA. References to specialized literature on related models can be\nfound in Charles E. McCulloch [2008], though we have not found a model that would\napply directly. Derivation of such a model is one of the contributions of this work.\n4.2. Quantiﬁcation with Effect Size Conﬁdence Interval\nWe propose to construct a conﬁdence interval for the ratio of mean execution times.\nThis conﬁdence interval will be a measure of uncertainty of the metric that is of ul-\ntimate interest. Reported results can thus be for instance that system A is 4%±1.5%\nfaster than system B, with 95% conﬁdence. Multiple intervals would also lend them-\nselves easily to graphical visualisation. The method gives us a way to rigorously com-\npare against a threshold. Say that we only care about differences larger than 3%. Here,\nwe would conclude that performance of two systems is different if the upper bound of\nthe conﬁdence interval is less than 0.97 or its lower bound greater than 1.03,\nNote also that the method still allows a signiﬁcance-based binary decision. We con-\nclude that the systems “may have the same performance” if the interval includes 1.\nOtherwise, we would conclude that the systems are “likely not to have the same perfor-\nmance”. The chance of erroneously concluding that the systems differ in performance\nhere is the conﬁdence of the interval (5% for a 95% conﬁdence interval). Hence, even\nfor the signiﬁcance based method only, we have the advantage over the recommended\npractice that we know this error. In summary, the conﬁdence interval for the ratio of\nmeans subsumes the currently best recommended practice.\nConstructing a conﬁdence interval for a metric that measures the difference in two\nsystems is a known concept in statistics. Such a metric is called the effect size and\nhence the effect size conﬁdence interval. Effect size conﬁdence intervals have been pro-\nposed for quantiﬁcation as a replacement for signiﬁcance methods in psychology [Co-\nhen 1994], education [Coe 2002], medicine, bio-medicine, and biology [Nakagawa and\nCuthill 2007]. The change in statistical methods is not smooth, but evidence can be\nfound that effect size is already being used, at least to some extent, in medicine [Bland\nand Altman 2000] and psychology [Dunleavy et al. 2006]. To support the change, the\nuse of effect size has sometimes been made an ofﬁcial requirement (Hill and Thompson\n[2005] list 23 journals mostly in psychology and education that require reporting effect\nsize, and the American Psychological Association [2001] requires it in its Publication\nManual).\n2In statistical texts, one can say also ‘n factors’, but we refrain from doing so here to avoid confusion with\nthe informal meaning of a ‘factor’ used throughout the text. In our text, a ‘factor’ refers to a technical/real\ncause that may impact performance and a ‘way’ refers to a statistical model of (some of) such causes.\n\n\n=== PDF page 13 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n13\nThere are different metrics used for measure the effect size in different ﬁelds and\none of them is the ratio of means. There are statistical methods for interval construc-\ntion, although they did not make it to introductory textbooks (more information and\nreferences can be found in [Beyene and Moineddin 2005; Schaarschmidt 2007; Dilba\net al. 2007]). From several available methods, we have a closer look at two. One is\nbased on statistical simulation (bootstrap) [Davison and Hinkley 1997]. The other is\nbased on Fieller’s theorem [Fieller 1954], which gives a conﬁdence interval for the ratio\nof means of normally distributed variables. We show how both methods can be applied\nin the experimental design we propose, and empirically evaluate them.\n4.3. Related Efforts\nSome of the things we propose here have been proposed earlier in computer science,\nbut have not been widely adopted.\nA bootstrap-based method for construction of conﬁdence intervals is being used in\nthe Haskell community, supported by the Criterion benchmarking library [O’Sullivan\n2009]. The library is for measuring performance of one system only in single-level\nexperiments. Apart from using robust methods it has also the advantage of detecting\noutliers and auto-corellation of the data. The tool is based on [Boyer 2008].\nConﬁdence interval for speed-up has been proposed by Luo and John [2004] in\nthe context of processor simulation. The uncertainty does not come from random ef-\nfects, but rather from random sub-sampling – only randomly chosen execution inter-\nvals from the whole application execution are simulated, the same intervals in both\nof the systems to compare. The conﬁdence interval proposed for this problem comes\nfrom [Cochran 1977], it is a parametric interval based on asymptotic normality of not\nonly the means, but also their ratio, which is only possible for large samples. The\nmethod by Fieller [1954] that we use in this work is more general.\n5. OUTLINE OF THE NEW METHOD\nThe rest of our paper includes a detailed description of our quantiﬁcation method and\nits empirical evaluation based on real benchmark results. We offer two alternative\ndescriptions of our method. Section 6 provides a guide for practitioners, while Sec-\ntion 7 is a variant for scientists with some statistical background. Section 6 describes\nthe method in practical terms and with recommendations on how it can be used. Sec-\ntion 7 formulates the method in statistical terms, gives the assumptions and necessary\nproofs, and discusses further alternatives. We use the terminology common in the ﬁeld.\nIn statistical terms, the core of the method is our statistical model of random effects\nin n-way classiﬁcation, which models performance in one system. We apply it indepen-\ndently to both systems we aim to compare. For clarity, the model is described for 2-way\nclassiﬁcation ﬁrst (3 levels in the experiment, Section 7.1.1), but later in the general\nform of n-way classiﬁcation (an arbitrary number of levels, Section 7.1.2). While we\nkeep referring to ‘execution time’ in the statistics to make presentation concrete, the\nmodel is sufﬁciently general for any response variable, given the stated assumptions.\nWe then show how to construct the conﬁdence interval for the mean within this model,\nstill for the mean of each system separately (Section 7.2). We give two alternative\nmethods, a parametric method based on asymptotic normality and a non-parametric\nmethod based on bootstrap. Later we show how to construct the conﬁdence interval for\nthe ratio of the means of two systems, again using a parametric and non-parametric\nmethod (Section 7.3). The non-parametric bootstrap method is a natural extension of\nthe method for a single system. The parametric method uses Fieller’s theorem [Fieller\n1954].\n\n\n=== PDF page 14 ===\n14\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\n6. FOR PRACTITIONERS: THE NEW METHOD WITHOUT STATISTICS\nThe technique we propose here comprises of the actual statistical method (proven and\nprecisely deﬁned in the following section) and of practical advice how to use it. We feel\nthat it is important to stress that while the statistical method is general, our advice\nmay not be. The practicalities depend on the context (the goal of the study, the critical-\nity of the results and the consequences of possible errors, the kind of computer system\nevaluated, the runtime environment, infrastructure, and benchmarks). We understand\nthat having a single best technique that could be mechanically followed in every study\nmight be appealing, but we strongly believe that such practice is an illusion, and we\ndeﬁnitely do not provide one here.\n6.1. Designing the Experiments\nWe describe our technique sequentially, but in practice it would be an iterative process.\nAt the end of this section, we provide a small worked example.\nInitial number of levels. When planning experiments, we ﬁrst decide what the high-\nest level of the experiment should be. All factors inﬂuencing performance above that\nlevel need to be controlled (and documented). The highest level might be compilation\n(of the benchmark, runtime environment, or all). If compilation is found to be fully\ndeterministic, this may not be needed. Though note that even symbol names have im-\npact on performance [Gu et al. 2004], so we may choose to randomize these or otherwise\nrandomize the building process to get rid of other sources of measurement bias.\nOn the other hand, in practice, if repeating say compilation is very expensive, and\nwe know that it has relatively little impact on performance or we ﬁnd it out during\nthe following process, we may choose not to repeat it. The threshold we then use for\ncomparisons, however, needs to be set accordingly.\nAfter deciding on the highest level, we continue by identifying lower levels. We need\nto add an additional lower level to the experiment if the variability (say in executions)\nis expected or known to be higher than in the level above (say compilations), while the\ncost of repeating at the lower level is smaller than at the higher level. If we create a\nlevel where it is in fact not needed, we may detect this later in the following process.\nAlso, we can measure the costs of repetition in the following process as a side-effect\n(for example, such cost is the time to build a new binary). In the formulae below, the\nnumber of levels is n+1 (where n is the number of ‘ways’ in the statistical model, which\nis one less than the number of levels in the experiment).\nNumbers of repetitions. Having decided on initial levels of the experiment, we run\nthe experiment a few times to estimate variability at different levels. With our method,\nwe always use the same repetition counts at a particular level (thus all executions have\nthe same number of measurements, all binaries same number of executions, etc.). We\ndenote the repetition counts as ni, where 1 ≤i ≤n + 1 is the level of the experiment\n(nn+1 is the number at highest level, say compilation).\nFor the initial experiment, we can set all these counts to the same value, say 30.\nNote that these counts do not include warming-up various components of the experi-\nment. Usually, we want to evaluate only measurements from a steady-state of every\nexecution. Say that we do so by discarding the ﬁrst c1 measurements of each execution\nbefore collecting the n1 we need. c1 must be found by the experimenter. Repetitions at\nhigher levels, ci, 1 < i ≤n, also may have a non-zero cost. For instance, compilation\nhas notable cost as it takes time to compile. All costs are represented as counts of mea-\nsurements — the cost of compilation is the compilation time divided by the (average)\ntime for a measurement. Precision here is not crucial, as the costs are only needed for\noptimisation.\n\n\n=== PDF page 15 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n15\nTo run the initial experiment, we need to have c1 already and we use ni = 30. During\nthe experiment we collect measurements and also measure the costs for repetition ci\nfor 1 < i ≤n. Based on the costs and the measurements, we decide on optimum counts\nof repetitions at all but the highest level (the derivation is provided in Sections 7.4\nand 7.5) :\nn1 =\n& s\nc1\nT 2\n1\nT 2\n2\n'\n,\n∀i, 1 < i ≤n ni =\n& s\nci\nci−1\nT 2\ni\nT 2\ni+1\n'\n.\nWe obtain Ti, the unbiased estimator of the variance at level i, through an iterative\nprocess. First, we calculate Si, the biased estimator of the variance at level i (formulae\nbelow). This is semanti-\ncally equivalent of running the experiment all again with fewer levels, but we obtain\nthe same effect by dropping data from the repetitions. We calculate Sis as follows (the\nused notation is detailed in Sections 7.1.2 and 7.1.3)\nS2\n1 =\n1\nQn+1\nk=2 nk\n1\nn1 −1\nnn+1\nX\njn+1=1\n. . .\nn1\nX\nj1=1\n\u0000Yjn+1...j1 −Y jn+1...j2•\n\u00012\nfor i, 2 ≤i ≤n\nS2\ni =\n1\nQn+1\nk=i+1 nk\n1\nni −1\nnn+1\nX\njn+1=1\n. . .\nni\nX\nji=1\n\nY jn+1...ji • . . . •\n| {z }\ni−1\n−Y jn+1...ji+1 • . . . •\n| {z }\ni\n\n\n2\nand ﬁnally\nS2\nn+1 =\n1\nnn+1 −1\nnn+1\nX\njn+1=1\n\nY jn+1 • . . . •\n| {z }\nn\n−Y • . . . •\n| {z }\nn+1\n\n\n2\nY denotes the measurements, indexed by experiment levels (highest to lowest). The\nbar over Y denotes an arithmetic mean. The mean is always calculated over all the\nindexes that are denoted by a bullet. While the formulas for Sis may seem involved in\nfull generality, it is easy to see how they are constructed. Suppose we have functions\nM and V that calculate sample mean and variance of a vector,\nM(x1 . . . xk) = 1\nk\nk\nX\ni=1\nxi\nV (x1 . . . xk) =\n1\nk −1\nk\nX\ni=1\n(xi −M(x1 . . . xk))2\nAny statistical software would have these functions. For n = 2, each of S2\n1, S2\n2, and S2\n3\nis created by three applications of these functions to the data, where V is applied once\nand M two times, but always in different order (see Table II). Each application of M\nor V reduces the dimension of the data. For example, to calculate S2\n2 we ﬁrst apply\nM on measurements from each execution, hence getting a two-dimensional matrix\n(binaries × execution means) from a three-dimensional matrix (binaries × executions\n× measurements). Second, we apply V on the execution means, getting a vector (one\n\n\n=== PDF page 16 ===\n16\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\nTable II. Third, we apply M on this\nvector and get S2\n2.\nIf we obtain a small number of repetitions at some level, we may decide to remove\nthat level to simplify the experiment. Indeed for practical purposes we can also in-\ncrease the number of repetitions at the lowest level (i.e. measurements), if a measure-\nment is very short. As there is no further overhead for additional measurements at\nthe lowest level (since we do not have to repeat higher levels and nor perform further\nwarm-up), it is cheap to get more data useful for other things, such as spotting unusual\nbehaviour. This method would not work well for very small repetition numbers (say,\nbelow 5, but this depends on experimenter’s judgment) — if at all possible, we should\nnot use such small counts. Note that in practice, to make the process simpler at the\ncost of more expensive experiments in the end, we could assume Ti = Si. In that case\nwe would not get problems with the negative numbers, but the resulting numbers of\nrepetitions at higher levels may be higher than needed.\nOnce we have decided on the ﬁnal number of levels and repetition counts, we re-run\nthe experiments and continue as follows. In the following, ni and n refer to the new set-\nting. In practice, the established setting would be re-used for very similar experiments\n(same platform, benchmarks, etc).\nExample. Let us assume that we are to dimension an experiment based on three-\nlevel data (n = 2) shown in Table III (Raw Data). We have measurements from 3\nbinaries, each executed 2 times, and reporting 2 measurements per each execution. In a\nreal experiment we will have more, but this is to demonstrate the method. To calculate\nthe T 2\ni we need the S2\ni , which we will calculate iteratively as demonstrated in the table.\nFirst, we calculate the (matrices of) execution means and variances (• • M and • • V ),\nas recorded in the second and third table. Next, we calculate the column means and\nvariances of • • M, getting vectors •MM and •V M. Also we calculate •MV . Finally,\nwe get S2\n3\n.= 3.6 (V MM), S2\n2\n.= 2.6 (MV M), and S2\n1\n.= 16.5 (MMV ). The grand mean,\nthe mean of all measurements, is 6.5 (MMM). We now obtain the Ti: T 2\n1 = S2\n1\n.= 16.5,\nT 2\n2 = S2\n2 −S2\n1/2 .= −5.7, and T 2\n3\n.= 2.3. As we ﬁnd that T 2\n2 < 0, we can remove the second\nlevel of the experiment. Hence, we only will run one execution per binary.\nLet us also suppose that we observed that the system stabilises running a single\nexecution reliably after 10 measurements (c1 = 10, suppose that Table III already con-\ntains only the stable measurements). We need to ﬁnd the optimum repetition counts.\nAs we decided to remove the second level of the experiment, this optimum repetition\ncount is just the number of measurements per execution (the number of executions per\nbinary will always be 1, and the number of binaries we can increase any time to get\nmore precise results). To ﬁnd the optimum we need to recalculate the T 2\ni and hence\nthe S2\ni for two-level experiment (n = 1). Applying the same method as before, we get\nS2\n2\n.= 3.6 and S2\n1\n.= 12.7 (Table IV), hence T 2\n1\n.= 12.7 and T 2\n2\n.= 0.4. Summarizing the Results\nQuantifying Performance of One System. We report performance of one system (in\nisolation) as the arithmetic mean of all measurements Y (we omit the bullets in the\nnotation for simplicity). We estimate its uncertainty using a (1−α) conﬁdence interval\n(i.e. α = 0.05 gives a 95% conﬁdence interval):\nY ± t1−α\n2 ,ν\ns\nS2\nn+1\nnn+1\n= Y ± t1−α\n2 ,ν\nv\nu\nu\nu\nt\n1\nnn+1(nn+1 −1)\nnn+1\nX\njn+1=1\n\nY jn+1 • . . . •\n| {z }\nn\n−Y • . . . •\n| {z }\nn+1\n\n\n2\nwhere t1−α\n2 ,ν is the 1 −α\n2 -quantile of the t-distribution with ν = nn+1 −1 degrees of\nfreedom. The mathematical background is given in Section 7.2.2.\nAlternatively, we can use statistical simulation (bootstrap) to calculate the conﬁ-\ndence interval. Say that we perform 1000 steps (or more if there is time). Within each\nstep, we use the real data to simulate a new experiment. First, at the highest level, we\nrandomly decide which iterations of the real experiment to use (e.g. which binaries).\nWe generate the same number of iterations as the real experiment, but some of the\nreal iterations can be used multiple times while some not be used at all. We then apply\nthis principle to lower levels. In the end, we get the same number of measurements\nas in a real experiment, with the same structure (repetition counts at each level and\nnumber of levels), and we calculate a sample mean of all these measurements. Hence,\nwe get 1000 means, each originating from one step of the simulation.\nWe form a (1 −α) conﬁdence interval for the mean using α/2 and 1 −α/2 sample\nquantiles of these means. If we have 1000 steps, we can do this by ordering the sim-\n\n\n=== PDF page 18 ===\n18\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\nTable V. Example Raw Data (Three-level)\nRaw Data - Old System\n• • •\nBinaries\nExecutions\n9 11\n16 13\n15\n7\n5\n6\n12\n8\n10 14\nRaw Data - New System\n• • •\nBinaries\nExecutions\n10 12\n9 1\n8 5\n6\n7\n11 4\n3 2\nulated means and taking the 25th and 975th values. Pseudo-code that illustrates this\nprocedure is shown in Figure 1 on page 26 and more details are given in Section 7.2.1.\nQuantifying Performance Change. To quantify a performance change of a ‘new’ sys-\ntem over an ‘old’ system, we use the same numbers of repetitions and levels for both.\nWe report the ratio of mean execution times of the two systems. As a measure of un-\ncertainty, we report a (1−α) conﬁdence interval (again α = 0.05 gives a 95% conﬁdence\ninterval) as follows. For detailed notation and derivation, see Sections 7.3 and 7.3.2.\nOY · NY ∓\ns\u0010\nOY · NY\n\u00112\n−\n\u0012\u0010\nOY\n\u00112\n−t2α\n2 ,ν ·\nOS2\nn+1\nnn+1\n\u0013 \u0012\u0010\nNY\n\u00112\n−t2α\n2 ,ν ·\nNS2\nn+1\nnn+1\n\u0013\nOY\n2 −t2α\n2 ,ν · OS2\nn+1 · nn+1−1\nThe term t α\n2 ,ν denotes the α\n2 -quantile of the t-distribution with ν = nn+1 −1 degrees of\nfreedom. The left-super-scripts ‘N’ and ‘O’ denote the new and the old system that we\ncompare.\nAlternatively, we can use statistical simulation (bootstrap) to calculate the conﬁ-\ndence interval. The algorithm is similar to the case of one system. We again perform a\nnumber of steps (say 1000), each producing the metric of interest from a simulated ex-\nperiment, which now is the ratio of means. Hence, in each step, we simulate measure-\nments from both systems, calculate their sample means, and then their ratio. When\nwe have these 1000 ratios, we take the sample quantiles for the conﬁdence interval\n(i.e. 25th and 975th for a 95% interval). Variances at the\nhighest level are simply variances of means (of binaries, in our case). For the old sys-\ntem, the mean for the ﬁrst binary is 7.8, for the second 12.3 and for the third 11.5 (all\nnumbers rounded to one decimal place). Conﬁdence Interval for the Mean of One System\nWithin the model described in the Section 7.1, we can construct a conﬁdence interval\nfor the mean execution time (µ) of a single system. In Section 7.3, we extend this to the\nconﬁdence interval for the ratio of means of two systems.\nWe show how to construct the interval using two alternative methods. The bootstrap\nmethod is intuitively simple and works for additional metrics, such as the median,\nas well as the mean. The parametric method based on asymptotic normality of the\n\n\n=== PDF page 25 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n25\nmodel (shown in Section 7.1), only works for the mean, but has the potential to provide\nnarrower intervals for larger sample sizes. We compare the two methods empirically\nin Section 8.\n7.2.1. Bootstrap Conﬁdence Interval for One System. Let us assume that we have n + 1\nlevels of hierarchy in our experiment and that we decided to repeat nn+1, nn, . . . n1\ntimes at each level. When we have completed benchmarking, we would thus have\nnn+1nn · . . . · n1 measurements, which we denote as\nYjn+1jn...j1\n(∀i, 1 ≤i ≤n + 1,\n1 ≤ji ≤ni)\nWe assume that the measurements from a single execution, that is Yjn+1...j2• for any\nﬁxed jn+1 . . . j2, are independent identically distributed, and we assume independence\nof means at higher levels as described in the previous section.\nVery informally, the core idea behind bootstrap is to simulate (many) experiments\nbased on the real data and then calculate what we want on the simulated data, in\nour case to calculate the conﬁdence interval. So instead of one realization of Y which\ncomes from our real data, we can have many realizations, generated by simulation,\nwhich is much faster than real experimentation. From these simulated realizations,\nwe construct the conﬁdence interval by selecting appropriate quantiles. Detailed dis-\ncussion and formulation of different bootstrap methods can be found in Davison and\nHinkley [1997].\nThe simulation of the new realizations of Y in each iteration randomly selects a\nsubset of real data (Y• . . . •\n| {z }\nn+1\n) with replacement. This means that the size of the subset\nwill be the same as of the original set (nn+1 · . . . · n1), but some data points may not\nbe present, while others can be presented multiple times. In each iteration, the sim-\nulation calculates a sample arithmetic mean of the subset. After all iterations ﬁnish,\nwe estimate the 0.025 and 0.975 quantiles for a 95% conﬁdence interval (if we have\n1000 iterations, the quantiles can be estimated as 25th and 975th ordered values, al-\nthough different estimators for quantiles exist). Pseudo-code for the described method\nis shown in Figure 1. For a 95% interval, we select α = 0.05.\nOut of the wide variety of bootstrap methods, the key decisions here were how to do\nresampling, and how to construct the conﬁdence interval given the simulated means.\nFor resampling, depending on the underlying distribution and the statistic of interest,\nit is sometimes better to resample with replacement only at higher levels of the hier-\narchy, but keep the lower levels intact [Ren et al. 2010; Davison and Hinkley 1997].\nA na¨ıve alternative is then also to ignore the structure of the model and resample at\nrandom from all measurements. We cover these alternatives in our evaluation later.\nThe method of resampling shown in Figure 1, replacement at all levels, seems to work\nbest (or at least not worse than others), and is a common default.\nFor the construction of the conﬁdence interval given the simulated means, we use\nthe percentile method. This method is sensitive to non-symmetrical distributions of the\nstatistic, but it should not be much of a problem here as we have shown earlier that\nY is asymptotically normal. Still, one can easily plug in alternative bootstrap methods\nfor conﬁdence interval construction [Davison and Hinkley 1997]. Statistical software\npackages, such as R, implement plenty of different methods.\n7.2.2. Asymptotic Conﬁdence Interval for One System. Alternatively to bootstrap, we can\nconstruct a conﬁdence interval for the mean with n-way classiﬁcation using the asymp-\ntotic normality of Y\n(equation 10 of Section 7.1.3). For this we need to estimate the\nunknown variance of the sample mean. It is easier to do this directly than by estimat-\ning the individual variances σ2\ni , 1 ≤i ≤n + 1.\n\n\n=== PDF page 26 ===\n26\nT. Kalibera, R.E. McCulloch [2008], including the respective conﬁdence inter-\nval, which uses the t-distribution with nn+1 −1 degrees of freedom. For large numbers\nof degrees of freedom, the t distribution converges to normal, so the choice is not im-\nportant. For small number of degrees of freedom, say smaller than 30 and deﬁnitely\nsmaller than 20, the conﬁdence intervals become wider with the t-distribution than\n\n\n=== PDF page 27 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n27\nwith the Normal distribution. This means that under the normality assumptions, one\nshould deﬁnitely use the t distribution, otherwise the interval would be too narrow\n(its coverage will be smaller than the projected 1 −α). In the practice, a larger than\nprojected coverage is usually regarded as better than a smaller one, so it makes sense\nto use the t distribution anyway.\n7.3. Conﬁdence Interval for Ratio of Means\nIn this section, we show how the method for constructing the conﬁdence interval for\nthe mean of one system (Section 7.2) can be extended to a conﬁdence interval of the\nratio of means of two systems. We will refer to these systems as ‘old’ and ‘new’, and\nmake the same set of assumptions for the two systems as we did for the single sys-\ntem so far (independence and identical distributions at multiple levels). We denote\nthe corresponding random variables for execution time as OY and NY , and the means\nas Oµ = E(OY ) and Nµ = E(NY ). Thus, we now have a bivariate distribution (F) of\nONY = (OY,N Y ) ∼F(xO, xN). We assume that OY and NY are independent. The vari-\nances Nσ2\ni , Oσ2\ni and expectations in the two systems can differ. Even the distributions\nmay differ. The parameter of interest is now\nθ = t(F) =\nNµ\nOµ =\nR\nxN dF(xO, xN)\nR\nxO dF(xO, xN)\nWe estimate θ using measurements of the two systems. Again, we use balanced mea-\nsurements and the same repetition counts (n1, . . . , nn+1) for both systems. It can be\nshown that T,\nT = t( bF) =\nNY\nOY\nis an unbiased (plugin) estimator for θ (Davison and Hinkley [1997], Example 2.2).\nHence, we can estimate θ using the ratio of arithmetic averages of the two systems,\nand we will do so both in the bootstrap and the asymptotic parametric method.\nWe estimate the unknown variances var(OY ) and var(NY ) by OS\n2\nn+1 and NS\n2\nn+1 from\nSection 7.1.\n7.3.1. Bootstrap Conﬁdence Interval for Ratio of Means. To construct a bootstrap interval\nfor the ratio of means θ, we need to simulate many realisations of its estimator T.\nα/2- and 1 −α/2- quantiles of these realisations form a (1 −α) percentile bootstrap\nconﬁdence interval for θ. The pseudo-code is shown in Figure 2. Discussion of more\nelaborate bootstrap methods for construction of the ratio of means can be found in von\nLuxburg and Franz [2009].\n7.3.2. Asymptotic Conﬁdence Interval for Ratio of Means. We can construct an asymptotic\ninterval for θ using a theorem by Fieller [1954], which gives conﬁdence limits for the\nratio of means of two normally distributed variables.\nLEMMA 7.7 (FIELLER’S THEOREM [FIELLER 1954]).\nLet X, Y be normally dis-\ntributed random variables, not necessarily independent. Let x, y be unbiased estimates\nof the means E(X), E(Y ). Let vxx, vyy be variances of x and y (note, not of X and Y ). Let\nvxy be sample covariance of x and y. Then, the conﬁdence limits for E(X)/E(Y ) are:\nα1, α2 =\n\u0000xy −t2vxy\n\u0001\n∓\nq\n(xy −t2vxy)2 −(x2 −t2vxx) (y2 −t2vyy)\nx2 −t2vxx\nwhere α1 is the lower limit (−sign) and α2 is the upper limit (+ sign). t is the critical\nvalue for the two-tail t-distribution, that is, for a 95% interval it is the 0.025th quantile\n\n\n=== PDF page 28 ===\n28\nT.
